Engineering Manager Take-Home
Problem: Job Management Dashboard
Introduction
This take-home assignment evaluates your skills in building a full stack prototype with AI tools.
We're looking for your ability to produce clean, well-structured code, understand how to use
generative AI, and then deploy that application.
While we do not expect engineering managers to write code day-to-day, the landscape of AI
agent software development capability is advancing quickly. At Rescale we want to ensure
leadership has a good understanding of the power and gotchas of such tools.
The problem should be completable within a few hours. Please focus on delivering a working
solution that meets the core requirements.
Problem Statement: Simple Job Management Dashboard
Rescale helps customers manage complex computational jobs. For this assignment, you will
build a simplified "Job Management Dashboard" where users can view, create, and manage the
status of computational jobs.
Core Requirements
1. Backend (Django & PostgreSQL)
○
Create a Django project with a single application.
○
Define a Job model with the following fields:
■
id: Primary key (automatically generated).
■
name: A string representing the job's name (e.g.,
"Fluid Dynamics
Simulation"
,
"ML Model Training").
■ created_at: Datetime field, automatically set on creation.
■ updated_at: Datetime field, automatically updated on each save.
○
Define a JobStatus model with the following fields:
■
id: Primary key (automatically generated).
■ job: A foreign key linking to the Job model.
■ status_type: A choice field with at least four distinct states (e.g.,
PENDING, RUNNING, COMPLETED, FAILED).
■ timestamp: Datetime field, captures when the status was recorded.
○
Implement a RESTful API with the following endpoints:
■ GET /api/jobs/: List all jobs. This endpoint should include the
current status for each job (i.e., the status_type from the latest
JobStatus entry for that job).
■
POST /api/jobs/: Create a new job. Upon creation, an initial
JobStatus entry (e.g., PENDING) should be automatically created for
this job.
■
PATCH /api/jobs/<id>/: Update a job. This action should involve
creating a new JobStatus entry for the job with the updated
status_type.
■
DELETE /api/jobs/<id>/: Delete a specific job. Ensure that all
associated JobStatus entries are also deleted.
○
Configure the Django application to connect to a PostgreSQL database.
2. Frontend (React & TypeScript)
○
Create a React application (e.g., using Create React App or Vite).
○
Display a list of all jobs fetched from your Django backend. Each job in the list
should show its name and current status.
○
Provide a form or input field to allow users to create a new job by entering a
name.
○
For each job in the list, provide a mechanism (e.g., a dropdown, buttons, or a
modal) to update its status to any of the defined states. This action should trigger
the creation of a new JobStatus entry on the backend.
○
For each job, provide a button to delete it.
○
Implement basic error handling for API calls (e.g., display a simple message if
fetching or updating fails).
○
Implement simple client-side validation for the new job form (e.g., name cannot
be empty).
○
Ensure the UI updates dynamically after creating, updating, or deleting jobs.
○
Style the application using a method of your choice to make the UI presentable.
3. Testing
○
Include at least one End-to-End (E2E) test using Playwright. This test should
cover a critical user flow, such as:
■ Creating a new job and verifying it appears in the list with the correct
initial status.
■ Updating a job's status to a different available status and verifying the
change.
4. Deployment & Setup
○
Provide Dockerfiles for both your Django backend and your React frontend
(or a single Dockerfile for the backend and instructions for serving the React
build).
○
Provide a docker-compose.yml file that orchestrates your Django backend,
PostgreSQL database, and React frontend, allowing for easy setup and running
of the entire application with a single command.
○
Include a Makefile with the following commands:
■
make build: Builds the Docker images.
■
make up: Starts the entire application stack using Docker Compose.
■
make test: Runs your Playwright E2E tests.
■
make stop: Stops the running Docker containers.
■
make clean: Removes Docker volumes/networks if necessary for a
clean slate.
5. Performance Considerations
○
While building the application, consider web performance optimization. Imagine a
scenario where there could be millions of jobs in the database.
○
Address how you would efficiently fetch and display this large dataset in the
frontend.
○
Briefly describe any performance considerations you made or optimizations you
implemented in your README.md file
6. Running a Job
○
For this exercise, you are not required to actually build the job scheduling
system.

Both planning docs are written (`docs/SPEC.md`, `docs/PLAN.md`). Plan mode is now on, so I'll hold off creating the rest until we settle the open questions — here's my understanding first.

## 1. Models & API, as I read them

**`JobStatus` is an append-only event log, not a mutable field.** The prompt's PATCH wording — "creating a *new* JobStatus entry" — makes that explicit. `Job.current_status` is therefore a *projection* of that log. Everything else follows from that framing.

- `Job`: `id, name, created_at, updated_at` + denormalized `current_status`, `current_status_at`
- `JobStatus`: `id, job (FK CASCADE), status_type (4 TextChoices), timestamp`
- `GET /api/jobs/` cursor-paginated with `current_status` inline · `POST` creates job + initial `PENDING` in one transaction · `PATCH` appends an event (also handles rename) · `DELETE` cascades · plus `GET /api/health/` so `make test` can wait for readiness instead of racing Postgres

## 2. Slices

11 vertical slices, each with one scoped Playwright spec. Slices 0–4 (health → list → create → patch → delete) are backend-only but still tested through Playwright's `request` fixture — same runner, same `make test`, no second framework. Slices 5–10 layer the UI on: list → create form → **status update (the prompt's required critical flow)** → delete → fault-injected error handling → pagination at scale. Slice 11 is README + a clean-machine `make test` rerun. Details in `docs/PLAN.md`.

## 3. On your two questions — I agree, with one refinement each

**Millions of jobs:** your reading is right, and I'd state it in the README as an explicit assumption: *the dataset is large, a single user's viewport is not.* The real job is making every query cost proportional to the page, not the table. Concretely that means cursor over offset pagination — `OFFSET 500000` makes Postgres walk and discard half a million rows, and DRF's page-number paginator additionally fires a `COUNT(*)` on **every** request, which is a full index scan per page load. Keyset pagination has neither problem.

**Current status:** agreed on denormalizing onto `Job` — the deciding factor is `?status=RUNNING`, which a derive-on-read subquery cannot serve without computing the latest status for every job in the table. One refinement: compare the incoming event against **`current_status_at`, not `updated_at`**. `updated_at` bumps on *every* save, so a rename would make a subsequent legitimate status event look stale and get silently dropped.

## 4. Things the prompt leaves open

Flagging the ones that don't need a decision from you — I'll note them in the README rather than build for them: **no auth/multi-tenancy** (the prompt never mentions users, so one shared job list); **no state-machine constraints** (prompt says update to *any* defined state, so `COMPLETED → RUNNING` is allowed); **repeat PATCH of the same status still appends an event**, since it's a log of observations, not a diff.

One gotcha worth surfacing now: the evaluation environment may only assume **DockerHub** access, but the standard Playwright image lives on `mcr.microsoft.com`, not DockerHub. I'll build the e2e image from `node:22-bookworm` instead. Getting that wrong fails `make test` on their machine, which per the prompt ends the evaluation.

Four decisions I'd like from you:

Rewritten. What changed:

**Validation is now explicit per slice**, on three tiers: **T1** lightweight (`make test-spec SPEC=…`, one spec against the running stack, seconds) after every change inside a slice; **T2** full suite at the close of *every* slice; **T3** cold gate (`make clean && make build && make test` from pruned Docker + amd64 check) at slices 0, 4, 7, 11 — plus any slice that touches Docker, compose, the Makefile, or a dependency, since that's the class of change that passes warm and breaks cold.

**You're right about #9.** Error handling is now built into each slice's definition of done — uniform DRF error shape in slice 1, serializer validation in 2, typed client + `ErrorBanner` in 5, form validation in 6, optimistic rollback in 7. What survives at position 9 is a *fault-injection sweep*: a spec that systematically proves the handling built in 1–8 holds under 500s, aborts, and slow responses. Building the handling early and verifying it systematically later is the right split; deferring the handling itself would have meant retrofitting it into eight slices.

**Two mermaid diagrams**: container topology (showing why the nginx `/api` proxy gives a single origin and kills CORS), and a sequence diagram of the status write — the one piece of non-obvious logic, where the log is *always* appended but the projection update is conditional.

**Test plan matrix: 34 cases, 15 negative**, grouped A–F (create / update / delete / list / scale / infra), each with action, expected behaviour, and the specific validation. Sign-off gate sits after slice 0 — the skeleton ships first so the plan is reviewed against something real, then you approve before feature work starts. A few worth your eye: **B8** (rename must *not* append a status event), **B9** (concurrent PATCHes — no lost update), **C4** (failed delete restores the row), **E2** (no duplicate or skipped ids across the cursor walk).

## What shipped

**Slice 0 — the gate.** `docker compose` with Postgres, Django/gunicorn, an nginx-served React build, and a run-once Playwright container. `make` gives you `build up test test-spec test-backend test-all seed stop clean logs time`.

**Slice 1 — models and the list endpoint.** `Job` + `JobStatus` with the composite indexes, `record_status()` as the single writer of the projection, cursor-paginated `GET /api/jobs/`, and `seed_jobs` for bulk-generating realistic status histories.

## Validation

| | Result |
|---|---|
| E2E suite (`make test`) | **12/12 pass** |
| Backend units (`make test-backend`) | **14/14 pass** |
| T3 cold gate — `make clean` then `make test` from pruned Docker | **pass** (F1, F2) |
| amd64 build — backend, frontend | **pass** (F4) |
| amd64 build — e2e image | still running; I'll report when it lands |

Two things worth knowing: `make test` **clears and re-seeds** the jobs table before running, which is what makes the suite deterministic and re-runnable without `make clean` — but it does mean it wipes local data. And the e2e image builds from `node:22-bookworm`, not `mcr.microsoft.com/playwright`, for the DockerHub reason.

**On your question:** `e2e` is the Playwright suite — so the tests were already in the diagram, just wearing an unhelpful name. It's `e2e/Dockerfile`: node + Chromium, containing `e2e/tests/*.spec.ts`. It sits behind a compose `test` profile, so `make up` never starts it; `make test` builds it, runs it once against the live stack, and it exits with the suite's status code. That packaging is what makes `make test` work on a machine with only make, docker, and bash.

Both diagrams now split into two subgraphs — **"the running application"** vs **"compose profile `test` — started only by `make test`, then exits"** — with the node labelled `e2e — Playwright runner` and the two dashed edges distinguishing UI specs (drives Chromium) from API specs (`request` fixture). Worth noting the API specs deliberately go through the same nginx origin rather than hitting Django directly, so they exercise the real proxy path.

**Transcript moved** to `docs/ai-session-transcript-2026-07-25.txt` (160K) and committed. It's the AI-usage deliverable, so it belongs with the other docs; the README will link it at slice 11.

[2026-07-25 15:45:52] USER
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Are the validatation tiers just ways to run all or a subset of the tests?

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 15:46:12] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Not quite — there are two different axes, and they're easy to conflate because T3 looks like "T2 again".

**T1 → T2 is a scope axis.** T1 runs one spec against the stack that's already up; T2 runs the whole suite. That one genuinely is "subset vs all" (plus `make test-backend`, which is a different suite entirely — pytest, not Playwright).

**T2 → T3 is not a scope axis at all.** They run the *identical* set of tests. What changes is the starting state: T3 wipes images, volumes, and the database first, then rebuilds from nothing. Same assertions, different thing under test — T2 validates the *application*, T3 validates the *build and provision path*.

That distinction matters because T3 catches a class of failure T2 structurally cannot:

- a file that exists in a stale image layer but was never committed to git
- a migration that only applies cleanly against an already-migrated database
- a dependency resolving from a warm cache that isn't actually pinned
- anything that depends on host state rather than repo state

Two concrete ones from this session: the initial migration got generated *inside* a container and never landed on the host, and `entrypoint.sh` was missing its exec bit locally — the Dockerfile's `chmod` masked it. Both were invisible warm and obvious cold. That's exactly the shape of "works on my machine," and it's the shape of failure the evaluation gate is built to catch, since they clone fresh and run `make test` once.

So the cheap/expensive tradeoff isn't really about test count — T3 costs several minutes because of image rebuilds and the Chromium download, not because it runs more assertions. Which is why the rule is positional (slices 0, 4, 7, 11) **plus** unconditional whenever Docker, compose, the Makefile, or dependencies change — that's the change class that passes warm and fails cold.

Want me to make that explicit in `TEST_PLAN.md` §2? It currently implies the distinction through the commands without stating it outright.

[2026-07-25 16:00:22] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Committed. Two substantive design questions — let me take them in turn.

**On `updated_at`: yes, and that's a real gap.** The C-group cases assert `current_status` and the log length but never that the job row's timestamps moved. That matters because `updated_at` and `current_status_at` are *supposed to diverge* in specific ways, and nothing currently pins that down:

| Action | `current_status` | `current_status_at` | `updated_at` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Status change | changes | **advances** | **advances** |
| Rename only (C8) | unchanged | **must not move** | **advances** |
| Stale/replayed event | unchanged | must not move | unchanged |

That middle row is the whole reason the guard compares against `current_status_at` instead of `updated_at` — so asserting it belongs in the E2E cases, not just the backend unit test where it currently lives. I'll add those assertions to C1, C4, and C8.

**On the state machine** — your instinct is right about the domain and I don't want to wave it away. `COMPLETED → RUNNING` is genuinely meaningless in a real scheduler; a re-run is a *new attempt*, not a backwards edit of the old one.

The complication is what the dropdown actually *is* here. The prompt excludes building a scheduler, so the status control isn't a user workflow action — it's a stand-in for the events a scheduler would emit. Under that reading, restricting it removes your only way to reach a state for demo or test purposes. And the prompt says the mechanism should update status "to any of the defined states," so a hard state machine means an evaluator clicking through the UI finds statuses they can't select, which reads as a bug rather than a design decision.

**On same-status-twice:** agreed it isn't a product feature. But I'd keep a thin version of the case rather than drop it, precisely for the reason you named — a double-submit or retry race *can* produce it, and the useful assertion is that it's harmless: appends an event, projection unchanged, timestamps advance, nothing corrupted. That's robustness, not a feature.

[2026-07-25 16:04:02] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Sharp catch — the D2 validation as written doesn't actually prove what it claims, and chasing it down surfaced an error in the spec.

**What D1's validation means** — two distinct things:
- *"no refresh needed"* → the row leaves the DOM without navigating (the prompt's "UI updates dynamically" requirement)
- *"still gone after reload"* → it was **persisted server-side**. A reload re-fetches from the API, so if the row reappears, the delete only happened in the client cache and the server never saw it. That's the failure this catches.

**Your real question — how do we prove the status rows are gone — exposes a hole.** `GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/` returning 404 proves almost nothing about the cascade: that route is nested under the job, so it 404s on the *parent* lookup. Orphaned `JobStatus` rows with a dangling `job_id` would produce a byte-identical 404. The assertion can't distinguish "cascaded" from "leaked".

Proving no orphans remain needs direct DB access, which E2E doesn't have — so it belongs in a backend unit test (`JobStatus.objects.filter(job_id=…)` empty). I'm splitting D2 into the reachability half and the orphan half, and saying explicitly why the E2E half is insufficient alone.

**And a correction to `SPEC.md`:** it claims cascade is "enforced at the DB level by the FK constraint… so it holds for raw SQL too." That's wrong. Django's `on_delete=CASCADE` is an *ORM-level* collector that issues its own `DELETE` statements; the Postgres constraint it emits has no `ON DELETE CASCADE` clause. A raw SQL `DELETE FROM jobs_job` would raise a foreign-key violation, not cascade.

[2026-07-25 16:05:49] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Done and committed. Summary of what changed from your three points:

**State machine → permissive, but named.** `jobs/transitions.py` will ship a permissive default *next to* the strict map it would enforce, with `POLICY = PERMISSIVE  # swap to STRICT`. Your domain instinct is right and it's now written down as such — the reason permissive wins here is that the prompt excludes building a scheduler, so the dropdown is standing in for scheduler events rather than modelling a user workflow. Lock it down and you can't reach states to demo or test them, and the prompt explicitly asks for a control reaching "any of the defined states" — greyed-out options read as a bug. New case **C10** unit-tests the strict map so it's functional code, not a decorative comment. Recorded as **Q7**.

**Same-status-twice** kept but reframed exactly as you put it: an ordinary update, API-level test only, robustness against a double-submit or retry race rather than a product feature. Recorded as **Q8**.

**`updated_at` assertions added** to C1, C4, and C8 — and this turned out to be the most valuable of the three, because the two timestamps are *supposed* to diverge and nothing was pinning that down. C8 now asserts a rename advances `updated_at` but leaves `current_status_at` untouched, which is the precise reason the guard compares against `current_status_at`.

**Your D1 question surfaced a real defect.** D1's validation is two claims: the row leaves the DOM without a refresh (dynamic-update requirement), and it's still gone after a reload (proving the delete reached the server, not just the client cache).

But your follow-up — how do we validate the status rows are gone — exposed that **D2 never proved the cascade at all**. `GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/` is nested under the job, so it 404s on the *parent* lookup; orphaned rows would return a byte-identical 404. Now split into **D2** (unreachable via API) and **D3** (backend unit: no orphan rows), since detecting orphans needs direct DB access.

Chasing that also caught a factual error in `SPEC.md`: it claimed cascade was "enforced at the DB level… so it holds for raw SQL too." It isn't — Django's `on_delete=CASCADE` is an ORM-level collector, and the Postgres constraint it emits has no `ON DELETE CASCADE` clause. Raw SQL would hit a foreign-key violation instead. Corrected, with the one-line `RunSQL` hardening noted as what would make the original claim true.

Plan is now **42 cases, 14 negative**. Backend tests still 14/14. Two open items for you: the sign-off itself, and whether you want that `RunSQL` DB-level cascade added in slice 4 — I've left it out as prototype-appropriate, but it's cheap if you'd rather have the belt-and-braces.

    ← DRF 3.15.2 | (offset, reverse, current_position) = (0, False, None) | (offset, reverse, current_position) = self.cursor | if current_position is not None: | order = self.ordering[0] | kwargs = {order_

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 16:08:14] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Confirmed, and it turned up something worth knowing. DRF 3.15.2's cursor builds its predicate from **`self.ordering[0]` only** — `created_at` — and handles rows sharing that value with an integer `offset`, not with the `id` tiebreaker.

So my `-id` tiebreaker makes the SQL `ORDER BY` total (which is what stops nondeterministic ordering), but it does **not** enter the cursor predicate. Within a group of rows sharing an identical `created_at`, DRF falls back to offset-based paging — and that's precisely where a mid-walk deletion can skip a row. Your test idea targets the one weak spot in the scheme.

[2026-07-25 16:10:46] USER
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme: `prefers-color-scheme` carries the OS preference, and the viewer's toggle stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, which must override the media query in both directions. The robust pattern is token-level: define the palette as custom properties on `:root`, redefine only the tokens under `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` — style components through the tokens, never directly inside the media query — then redefine them again under `:root[data-theme="dark"]` and `:root[data-theme="light"]`. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles** 

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment. 
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content. 
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated. 
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Design plan before building:

**Palette** — the chrome is near-monochrome cool slate; the *only* saturated colour on the page is job status. That's the core information-design decision: status pops because nothing competes with it. Interactive accent is a periwinkle `#4b57c8` / `#8f9cff` chosen specifically because it reads as neither running-cyan nor completed-green nor failed-red.

**Type** — no webfonts (CSP blocks CDNs, and a silent fallback is worse than a system stack). Character comes from role separation instead: job names in the UI sans, but every machine value — ids, timestamps, status labels — in uppercase mono with tabular numerals. Scheduler-output vernacular, drawn from the domain rather than applied to it.

**Layout** — dense hairline-divided rows, not rounded cards with accent rails. A job queue is scanned, and rows are what scan well at the scale this thing claims to handle.

**Mockup is live: https://claude.ai/code/artifact/4c5b63ba-db49-49f0-b561-d648d06cdde8**

The top half is a working prototype — search, filter, create with validation, status menu, expandable history, delete, load more. Flip **Simulate API failure** and the next status change or delete fails, so you can watch the optimistic badge roll back and read the actual error copy rather than take my word for it. Below that, a gallery pins each remaining state (loading, fetch failure, create rejected with the typed name preserved, delete dialog, end of list), each tagged with the test-plan case it satisfies.

Three design decisions worth agreeing or overruling now, since they're expensive to reverse later:

**Status is the only saturated colour on the page.** Chrome is near-monochrome slate; the periwinkle accent was picked specifically because it reads as neither running-cyan, completed-green, nor failed-red. Status pops because nothing competes with it. Colour is never the sole carrier either — every state has a distinct uppercase label.

**Rows, not cards.** Hairline-divided rows with machine values (ids, timestamps, status labels) in tabular mono, job names in the UI sans. A job queue is scanned, not read, and this is the shape that stays scannable at the scale we're claiming to handle.

**The footer says "75 loaded", not "75 of 1,284."** That's cursor pagination showing through — there's no total because computing one means a `COUNT(*)` per page load. If you want a total, it has to be an approximation on a separate cached endpoint, and I'd rather you make that call now than discover the absence in slice 10.

Two things I'd flag as most likely to need your input: the **history disclosure** is currently a `▸ 3 events` button per row — cheap, but it competes with the status control for attention. And **delete has no undo**, only a confirm dialog; an undo toast would be friendlier but needs a soft-delete or a client-side buffer, which is real scope.

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 16:18:21] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
You didn't misunderstand the intent — my option text said "all transitions allowed," so the "policy named and explicit" label oversold what was actually being chosen. Your call is clear now: strict in the dropdown, terminal states can't be edited.

One consequence worth deciding before I implement, because it changes both the UI and the API. Strict means `COMPLETED` and `FAILED` become dead ends — once a job finishes, its status can never change again. That's honest domain modelling, but it also collides with the prompt's "update its status to **any** of the defined states," and an evaluator clicking a completed job finds every option greyed out with no way forward. That reads as broken unless there's a visible way out.

An explicit **Re-run** action resolves it: the dropdown refuses backwards edits, but a separate labelled button moves a finished job to `PENDING`. No silent backwards transitions, every state still reachable, and it matches how real schedulers work — a re-run is a deliberate act, not a status edit.

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 16:24:19] USER

    ← b87e35e design: enforce strict status transitions; retry failures only

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 16:24:29] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
You'd read the design option correctly — my label said "policy named and explicit" but the body said all transitions allowed, so the summary oversold it. Now reversed and enforced. Committed as `b87e35e`; mockup updated at the same URL.

**Transitions** — `PENDING → {RUNNING, FAILED}`, `RUNNING → {COMPLETED, FAILED}`, both terminal states rejecting edits with a 400. The dropdown shows every state but disables the illegal ones with a "not allowed" note, so the rules are legible rather than invisible.

**Re-run only from FAILED**, per your second message — a completed job shows *"Completed — no further changes"* next to Delete, rather than a dead button with no explanation. Re-running a completed job is a 400, not a hidden affordance.

Two consequences worth your eye, since both are behaviour changes rather than mechanical follow-through:

**Same-status-twice flipped from 400 to an idempotent 200 no-op.** A strict map has no self-edges, so a literal reading would reject a double-click as an invalid transition. That's hostile for what's nearly always a double-submit or a retried request. This reverses Q8 — under the permissive model a duplicate was an observation worth logging; under a workflow model it's timeline noise.

**Search is server-side across the whole table.** In the mockup it filters loaded rows, because there's no backend — I've labelled that in the UI so it doesn't read as intent. The real one has to hit the database, otherwise searching "combustor" returns nothing when the match is on page 400, and the user concludes it doesn't exist. That's a wrong answer delivered quickly.

The catch: `name ILIKE '%term%'` **can't use a btree index** — a leading wildcard forces a sequential scan, precisely the shape that dies on millions of rows. So it needs a `pg_trgm` GIN index. That ships with the `postgres:16` image, so it's a migration rather than a deployment prerequisite. Added **F10** to prove it: seed 250k, search for a name known to live far outside page one, expect the match — an assertion a page-local filter cannot pass.

Plan is now **48 cases, 17 negative**. Next up is slice 2 (`POST /api/jobs/`), with slices 3–4 now carrying the transition enforcement.

[2026-07-25 16:31:58] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Done — status now appears once per row instead of twice. Same URL: **https://claude.ai/code/artifact/4c5b63ba-db49-49f0-b561-d648d06cdde8**

![Row layout](/var/folders/1t/2pzf8l8n1s91m08j0qjb7lw40000gn/T/claude-chrome-screenshots-rjSX5U/screenshot-1784997088276-0.jpg)

Name leads (it's what you scan for), status sits right where the eye lands before the controls, and **Edit** turns that badge into a select *in place*. Illegal transitions are disabled inside the select, so the state machine is visible at the point of use rather than hidden in a separate menu. That also let me delete the custom dropdown entirely in favour of a native `<select>` — fewer moving parts, and keyboard/screen-reader behaviour comes free.

I drove it in Chrome rather than trusting the markup, which was worth doing — it confirmed the flows and caught three bugs:

- A `RUNNING` job offers Completed/Failed with **Pending disabled**; picking Completed advanced the badge, grew the log 2→3, and flipped the row to terminal.
- **Re-run** on the failed job returned it to Pending and grew the log 3→4, preserving the `FAILED` entry.
- Bugs fixed: **"1 events"** pluralization; and `.terminal-note`/`.scope-hint` had been collateral damage when I removed the dead menu CSS, so *"No further changes"* was rendering as body copy instead of a small label. The search hint also wrapped to three lines and now fits on one.

Worth a look when you review: the three row variants are visible together in the screenshot — `RUNNING` gets Edit, `COMPLETED` gets "no further changes", `FAILED` gets Re-run. That asymmetry is the design you asked for, so it's the thing to sanity-check.

Still open: the slice 5 sign-off (design + test plan), and whether you want the DB-level `ON DELETE CASCADE` in slice 4.

**Numbering fixed** — mockup is now **slice 1.5**, sitting where it was actually built. UI work reverts to 5–8, fault injection 9, scale 10, delivery 11; cold gates back to 0/4/7/11. The terminal list also had two competing "Slice 5" entries, which I've merged into one.

**ID has its own column**, with a header row above the list — five unlabelled columns was asking you to infer what each one was. Every column is now fixed-width and left-aligned so IDs, event counts, and badges form clean vertical edges; **actions** is the single right-aligned column, so Delete lands in the same place whether the row shows Edit, Re-run, or "no further changes". I measured it in the browser rather than eyeballing: header and body edges match exactly, and all four columns align across every row.

**On the ID itself** — `BigAutoField` already scales to 9.2 × 10¹⁸, so digits were never a capacity problem; the 4-digit values were just unrealistic seed data. Now 7 digits, which is what a system that's run a few million jobs actually looks like.

The GUID question is worth separating from scale, though, because the real argument for it isn't capacity — it's **enumeration**. With sequential IDs and no auth (our A1 assumption), anyone can walk `/api/jobs/1`, `/2`, `/3`, and the ID itself leaks roughly how many jobs the platform has ever run. That's a genuine business-intelligence leak for a multi-tenant HPC product.

The catch is that random UUIDv4 as a primary key hurts exactly the thing this design optimizes for: inserts scatter across the index instead of appending, causing page splits and fragmentation, and the index roughly doubles in size. **UUIDv7** avoids that — time-ordered, so it keeps insert locality *and* is non-enumerable.

My recommendation is to stay with bigint for this prototype and note UUIDv7 in the README as the hardening step, since the leak only matters once auth and tenancy exist. But it's a one-migration change now versus an awkward one later — say the word if you'd rather switch.

## What each slice added

**Slice 2 — POST.** Creation routes through `create_job()` so the job and its first status event land in one transaction; a job can never exist with an empty log. Names are trimmed before the blank check, stored verbatim (unicode, emoji, and `<script>` round-trip unchanged), and aren't unique.

**Slice 3 — PATCH with the state machine.** `apply_status_change()` takes the row lock *before* reading the status the decision is based on — otherwise two concurrent requests could both validate against a stale value. `record_status()` stays the low-level writer and enforces nothing, because job creation and the seed command legitimately write states the machine would reject as *edits*. Jobs now advertise `allowed_transitions` and `can_retry`, so the UI can disable illegal options rather than let you pick something the server will reject.

**Slice 4 — DELETE + history.** The orphan check (D3) is a backend unit test, not E2E, for the reason you surfaced earlier: the nested `statuses` route 404s on the *parent* lookup, so orphaned rows would return a byte-identical 404. Only a direct query distinguishes them. I added a standing invariant too — every `JobStatus` points at a live job — which would catch a future delete path that bypassed the collector.

## Two harness fixes worth flagging

Both were cases where a test tier wasn't doing what it claimed. Specs were only baked into the e2e image at build time, so adding one required a rebuild — the T1 "lightweight" tier wasn't lightweight. They're now mounted. And `make test-backend` was testing a **stale image**; it now builds. Backend source is deliberately *not* mounted, so the image stays the artifact.

`CLAUDE.md` is in place with the per-slice loop, the DockerHub constraint, and the settled decisions — so those persist across sessions without restating.

Next up when you're back is **slice 5**, the first UI slice, building against the mockup you signed off. Nothing is blocked.

## What's where

```
backend/jobs/          ← the actual application (565 lines)
  models.py       82   Job, JobStatus, StatusType, indexes
  services.py     99   ★ the only writer of the projection
  transitions.py  77   ★ the state machine
  serializers.py  98   API shapes + validation
  views.py        73   the ViewSet, thin by design
  pagination.py   58   cursor pagination + why
  exceptions.py   60   one error shape for the whole API
  urls.py         12
  management/commands/seed_jobs.py   bulk seed for perf work
  tests/          35 unit tests

e2e/tests/             52 Playwright specs (817 lines)
frontend/src/          slice 0 shell only — the real UI starts at slice 5
docs/                  SPEC · PLAN · TEST_PLAN · OPEN_QUESTIONS
```

Everything else — `config/`, Dockerfiles, `nginx.conf`, `Makefile`, `docker-compose.yml` — is plumbing you can skim unless something breaks.

## Where to focus

**Read these three, in this order.** They contain every decision worth arguing about:

1. **`models.py`** — the shape of everything. 82 lines.
2. **`services.py`** — `record_status()` and `apply_status_change()`. This is the heart: the only place `current_status` is ever written.
3. **`transitions.py`** — the state machine as a data structure, not scattered `if`s.

Then **`serializers.py`** for one specific trick: `status` is a **write-only** field. It's not a column on `Job` — it's an *instruction to append to the log*. What comes back is `current_status`, the projection that append produced.

`views.py` is deliberately boring — if logic ever accumulates there, it belongs in `services.py`.

## What `current_status_at` is for

It's the timestamp of the `JobStatus` event that produced `current_status`. Three jobs, in descending order of how much they matter:

**1. It makes the projection self-describing.** `current_status = RUNNING` alone doesn't tell you *when* it started running. To render "Running since 09:31" without it, you'd query the log per row — an N+1 across the whole list. This one's used every render.

**2. It's the guard in `record_status()`** (`services.py:44`):

```python
if timestamp >= locked.current_status_at:
    locked.current_status = status_type
    locked.current_status_at = timestamp
```

An event older than the projection gets **appended to the log but doesn't move the projection** — so a late or replayed event can't drag a job backwards.

**3. It makes drift detectable.** `current_status_at == max(statuses.timestamp)` is a cheap consistency check. Without it you can't tell a correct projection from a stale one without diffing the whole log — which matters because the projection is a cache, and caches need a repair path.

### Why not just use `updated_at`?

This is the part worth understanding, because it looks like the obvious simplification and it's wrong. `updated_at` moves on **every** save — including a rename. Concretely:

| Time | Event | `current_status_at` | `updated_at` |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10:00 | job goes RUNNING | 10:00 | 10:00 |
| 10:05 | someone renames the job | 10:00 | **10:05** |
| 10:03 | a delayed `COMPLETED` event arrives | ? | ? |

Guarding on `updated_at`: is `10:03 >= 10:05`? **No — the completion is silently dropped.** A rename ate a real status change.

Guarding on `current_status_at`: is `10:03 >= 10:00`? **Yes — applied correctly.**

That's `test_a_rename_cannot_make_a_later_status_look_stale` in `test_services.py`, and case C10 in the E2E suite.

### One honest caveat

**The guard is inert today.** Timestamps are server-stamped and `select_for_update()` serializes writers, so events *can't* currently arrive out of order — condition #2 never fires in production as it stands. It costs one integer comparison and it's what makes the design correct the moment status events come from somewhere other than a person clicking a dropdown: a real scheduler emitting webhooks, a retried delivery, a backfill. Jobs #1 and #3 above earn their keep right now regardless.

Full reasoning is in `docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md` Q2 if you want the long version.

There it is. Watch the third line:

| | `current_status_at` | `updated_at` |
|---|---|---|
| after create | 18:30:48 | 18:30:48 |
| after → RUNNING | 18:30:50 | 18:30:50 |
| **after rename** | **18:30:50** | **18:30:53** ← moved |

The job has been RUNNING since **:50**. `updated_at` now says **:53**, because it recorded the rename. "Running since 18:30:53" would just be wrong.

## The underlying reason

The two fields answer different questions:

- **`updated_at`** — "when did this *row* last change?"
- **`current_status_at`** — "when did this job enter its *current state*?"

They give the same answer only as long as **nothing but a status change ever touches the row**. That holds right up until it doesn't.

Today the thing that breaks it is rename. But the structural problem is that `updated_at` is bound to the row, not to the status — so *any* field added to `Job` later breaks it: a priority, an owner, a description, tags, a favourite flag. And it breaks **silently**. Nothing throws; the UI just quietly starts showing rename times as start times, and you find out from a bug report about a job that "restarted itself" when someone edited its description.

So it's less "we need a second timestamp" and more "we need a timestamp that means what we're displaying." `updated_at` is a row-level audit field that happens to coincide with status changes in a system this small.

## You're right that it's contingent

If we dropped rename, `updated_at` *would* be sufficient today — and the prompt only specifies PATCH as a status update, so rename isn't strictly required. That'd be a legitimate simplification: one fewer column, one fewer concept.

I'd still keep `current_status_at`, for two reasons. It's 8 bytes a row, so the cost is noise. And it's what makes the projection **verifiable** — `current_status_at == max(statuses.timestamp)` is a cheap drift check, which matters because `current_status` is a cache, and a cache with no repair path is a liability at the scale we're targeting.

But that's a judgment call, not a fact, and it's yours to make. Want me to drop rename and collapse to `updated_at`? It's a small change now — serializer, one migration, three tests — and considerably more annoying after the UI is built on it.

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 18:36:01] USER
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ← === Python side === StatusType MRO : StatusType -> TextChoices -> Choices -> StrEnum -> str is an Enum : True is also a str : True member : StatusType.RUNNING | .value = RUNNING | .label = Running ===

──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
[2026-07-25 18:36:17] ASSISTANT
──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
So: **enum in Python, `varchar(16)` in Postgres.** In code you write `StatusType.RUNNING`, get autocomplete, and DRF rejects anything not in the enum (that's the 400 in case C7). The real question is why the *column* stores text rather than a smallint or a native Postgres `ENUM`.

## The three options

**Native Postgres `ENUM`** — 4 bytes, enforced by the database itself. Rejected because it's a migration trap: adding a value means `ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE`, and you can't **remove or reorder** values at all. You'd be shipping a schema change that's effectively irreversible, for four states that might well grow to five.

**`IntegerChoices` → smallint** — 2 bytes, the compact option. Rejected for two reasons:

- The API speaks strings regardless — you'd never put `{"status": 2}` on the wire. So you'd map int↔string at the serializer boundary, and the database would no longer match the API. Every debugging session gains a translation step.
- `SELECT * FROM jobs_jobstatus` returns `status_type = 2`. In psql, in a log line, in a dump, in whatever BI tool someone points at this later — you'd need the code open to read your own data.

**`CharField` + `TextChoices`** — what's there. Readable at every layer, DB matches wire format exactly, and adding a state is a **no-op migration**: `choices` is Django-level metadata, so the column doesn't change.

## The honest cost

This isn't free, and it cuts against the scale theme:

| | per row | 10M jobs + 30M events |
|---|---|---|
| `varchar` ("COMPLETED") | ~10 bytes | ~400 MB |
| `smallint` | 2 bytes | ~80 MB |

It also widens the composite index `Job(current_status, created_at, id)` by roughly 30%.

I took that trade because a few hundred MB is cheap next to the operational cost of unreadable data, and because the *shape* of the query doesn't change — `WHERE current_status = 'RUNNING'` is an index seek either way, which is what actually matters at scale. Index **width** affects cache residency at the margin; index **usability** is what decides whether you scan 25 rows or 10 million.

If it ever did matter, the migration path is open in a way the native-`ENUM` one isn't: add a smallint column, backfill, swap. That's the reasoning behind the docstring in `models.py:22`.

`CANCELLED` is in and green: **57 E2E, 43 backend**. Committed as `dd95c9a`.

## On code review — one correction that helps you

`/code-review ultra` with **no arguments bundles your local branch** — it doesn't need a GitHub remote and doesn't need a push. So you're not blocked on pushing to review. The `<PR#>` form is the one that reads from GitHub.

Either way I can't launch it — it's user-triggered and billed, so it has to come from you.

That does make it a natural fit for the touchpoints, and I've added the cadence to `CLAUDE.md`. **You're at one right now**: slice 4 + CANCELLED, backend complete, T3 green.

## Worth knowing: nothing is pushed yet

`origin` is configured (`nina-jml/job-management-dashboard`) but **`origin/main` doesn't exist** — all **20 commits are local only**. Since the deliverable is a repo link, that's worth doing before you're relying on it.

I haven't pushed, since publishing to a remote is your call. Want me to `git push -u origin main`?

## What CANCELLED changed

The state machine is now:

```
PENDING   → RUNNING, FAILED, CANCELLED
RUNNING   → COMPLETED, FAILED, CANCELLED
COMPLETED → ∅                    (dead end — success)
FAILED    → ∅  + re-run          (didn't finish)
CANCELLED → ∅  + re-run          (didn't finish)
```

Two details worth your eye at review:

**The migration is a genuine no-op.** `sqlmigrate` emits `(no-op)` for both columns — adding a state cost zero schema change. That's the payoff of the CharField decision you asked about earlier; a native Postgres `ENUM` would have needed an irreversible `ALTER TYPE`.

**The retryable rule is now an invariant, not a list.** `test_every_retryable_state_is_terminal` asserts the *property* rather than enumerating states, so adding a sixth status can't quietly create two ways out of one state.

Pushing won't change it — `/code-review` reads your local branch either way. The remote isn't its input.

What actually determines the scope is your **branch structure**, and that's where the current setup is working against you: everything is on `main`, 20 commits, no branches. So there's no natural "changes since the last review" boundary for it to find — the diff base is ambiguous in a way it wouldn't be on a feature branch.

I don't want to guess at the exact base it picks. The review is running right now, so its output will tell us empirically what it covered — I'll say so plainly when it reports.

The practical fix for the cadence you described is a **branch per touchpoint**:

```
main ──● (slice 4 reviewed + pushed)
        └──● slice-5-ui ──● ──●   ← review scope is exactly this
```

That gives you three things at once: review scope that's precisely "since the last checkpoint", a PR you can run `/review` against, and a push that doesn't move `main` until you've approved it. It also matches how you described working — review and push at touchpoints, fast commits between.

If you want that, the moment to start is now: push `main` at this checkpoint (backend complete), then cut `slice-5-ui` for the frontend work. I'd hold off reorganising the 20 existing commits — they're already coherent one-per-slice, and rewriting history to retrofit branches would cost more than it returns.

## The three slices

**5 — job list.** Typed client where every failure (offline, HTTP, unparseable body) becomes one `ApiError`, so components render errors without knowing anything about fetch or DRF. Styling ported from the signed-off mockup rather than re-derived.

**6 — create form.** Validation runs twice deliberately: client-side so an empty name never becomes a request, server-side because the client isn't a trustworthy validator. The input clears only on success — a failed create that also loses what you typed is its own bug.

**7 — status update.** Optimistic badge with full rollback from a cache snapshot. It deliberately doesn't guess `allowed_transitions`/`can_retry` — those are the server's answer, and computing them client-side would be the second copy of the state machine this design has avoided throughout.

## Two things the tests caught that were real

**The filter chips were decorative.** They sent `?status=RUNNING` and the backend ignored it — DRF drops unknown query params silently, so filtering returned everything. Now implemented server-side against the indexed projection column, with an unknown status returning 400 rather than an empty list that reads as "no matches".

**T3 caught a genuine cross-slice regression.** Slice 5's history button had no accessible name beyond its visible text "History"; slice 7 added `aria-label="Edit status for <job name>"`, so a job named *"…History Job"* made a by-role query match two buttons. Fixed at the cause — the disclosure now has a stable label that never varies with data — rather than by making the selector cleverer.

## What's left

Slices 8–11: delete UI, fault-injection sweep, scale (250k seed + search, which needs the `pg_trgm` index), then README with the performance and prompt-engineering writeups.

One flag for whenever you pick this up: **search is in the UI plan but not yet in the API** — same shape as the filter bug, so I'll build the backend side first in slice 10.
